- **Detalle:** An educational Spanish-language video explains the rise of 0DTE (zero-day-to-expiration) S&P options, uses academic data to refute the 'fear theory' that they cause market jumps by showing market makers are usually long gamma and reduce intraday volatility, and teaches viewers how footprint absorption may reflect option hedging rather than directional conviction, ending with a call to comment for a free PDF.

Narrativa: This is an educational finance video in Spanish from Wisefox Traders x OMA x Adrianustrader, part of a series on 'las griegas' (the Greeks). Against a black background with minimalist gold line animations, the narrator explains that more than 50% of S&P 500 options now expire the same day they are traded (ODTE). In 2015, ODTE volume was under 20%; since daily expirations began in 2022, it exceeds 50%.

The video contrasts the popular 'fear theory'—that huge gamma causes massive hedging and market jumps—with academic data (Adams et al., 2026). Data shows market makers are long gamma 88% of 10-minute intervals because clients sell straddles and dealers buy them. With long gamma, hedging works against price moves (buying dips, selling rallies in ES futures), which reduces intraday volatility by about 7% on expiration days.

It illustrates how after a sharp ES drop, an order-flow imbalance appears in the E-mini but typically reverses within ~30 minutes. What looks like 'absorption' at a price level may simply be options hedging, not a directional buyer—volume show

- **Detalle:** An educational Spanish-language video explaining the options Greek 'charm' — how a dealer's delta decays over time causing end-of-day drift and pinning to strikes on expiration — using minimalist 3D animations and data references.

Narrativa: This is an educational finance video from Wisefox Traders x OMA x Adrianustrader, part 5 of a series on 'las griegas - el charm.' Using minimalist black-background animations of 3D line surfaces and clocks, it explains why 'the last hour of the ES moves by itself.' The narration states that without news or volume, price movement is driven by time decay. It defines theta as options losing value over time, then introduces 'charm' as the decay of delta (∂Δ/∂t). For dealers short far-dated puts who hedge by selling ES futures, the hedge's delta decays toward zero throughout the day, leaving excess short exposure that must be bought back ('recompra de futuros') in the afternoon — creating a 'drift' unrelated to price direction. The closer to expiration, the faster delta decays, so charm dominates in the last hour and on expiration days. Citing Golez and Jackwerth (Journal of Financial Economics, 2012), it shows that on option expiration days, the ES future 'pins' to the nearest strike due to at least $115 million per day of hedge rebalancing. When options expire, the hedge disappears and the 'magnet' turns off. The video summarizes this as drift, pin, and magnet-off, then teases Part 6 on ODTE (zero-day-to-expiration options) and prompts view

- **Fecha:** 2026-10-23
- **URL:** https://www.instagram.com/p/De1yuFSk3K8/
- **Likes:** 420 | **Comentarios:** 6
- **Detalle:** An 8-slide educational carousel from Quant Finance Institute explains Value at Risk versus Expected Shortfall with charts, definitions, limitations, and calculation methods aimed at aspiring quants.

Narrativa: This 8-slide Instagram carousel from QFI is an educational explainer comparing Value at Risk (VaR) and Expected Shortfall (ES). Slide 0 introduces the topic with a returns distribution showing Loss vs Profit and labels VaR and Expected Shortfall. Slide 1 defines VaR as the maximum expected loss at a confidence level over a time period, illustrating the 95% threshold. Slide 2 defines ES as the average loss beyond that VaR threshold, capturing tail risk. Slide 3 highlights VaR's key weakness: it says nothing about losses past the threshold, hiding the worst case. Slide 4 explains why ES is preferred—regulators increasingly require it because it captures tail risk directly, showing a 99% example ($384,912 VaR vs $435,761 ES). Slide 5 outlines Types of VaR Models: Historical Simulation, Parametric Model, and Monte Carlo Simulation, noting strengths and tradeoffs. Slide 6 shows a mock trading desk with Risk Monitor screens reinforcing why it matters, repeating the calculation methods. Slide 7 shifts to a lifestyle image of a video call and includes a call-to-action offering a full explanation to help get a quant job if users comment 'Risk'. The overall tone is professional, instructional, and promotional for quantitative finance education.

- **Likes:** 1400 | **Comentarios:** 780
- **Detalle:** An educational Spanish-language video explains how automated delta hedging by options dealers drives futures orders, showing that price moves are often formulaic calculations rather than human decisions.

Narrativa: This is an educational finance video (part 2 of 7, 'las griegas - el delta') from Wisefox Traders x OMA x Adrianustrader. Against a black background with minimalist 3D line-grid animations, it explains that many ES futures orders are not human decisions but automatic dealer hedging. When a client buys an S&P put, the dealer sells the put and hedges by buying/selling futures based on delta — e.g., delta 0.30 means 30 futures per 100 options. Because delta is not fixed, if ES falls the put's delta rises, forcing the dealer to sell more futures automatically, 'sin opinión, sin emoción.' The video cites academic research (Review of Financial Studies 2009, 2021; Journal of Financial Economics 2023) showing this rebalancing increases underlying volatility and can push futures in the day's direction in the last 30 minutes, then reverse over three days. Candles accelerating toward a level 'sin noticia' are usually hedging, not conviction. Whether hedging brakes or pushes depends on gamma — how fast delta changes — teased as Part 3. It closes by asking viewers to comment 'DELTA' to receive a free PDF explanation via DM.

- **Detalle:** A creator explains the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck stochastic process as a mathematical foundation for mean-reversion trading strategies, demonstrating the formula, its components, and a simple backtest on SPY showing outperformance versus buy-and-hold.

Narrativa: A man with glasses and a beard, seated at a desk with multiple monitors, delivers an educational video in Spanish about quantitative trading. He introduces the question of whether price is trending or temporarily out of equilibrium, displaying the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck equation dX_t = θ(μ - X_t)dt + σdW_t. He explains each term — price, speed of reversion (θ), long-term mean (μ), and volatility (σ) — and how the sign of (μ - X_t) pulls price back toward fair value. Using a Jupyter notebook titled 'Proceso de Ornstein-Uhlenbeck,' he shows simulations with different θ values ('Mismo ruido, distinta fuerza del imán') demonstrating faster reversion with higher θ. He then applies the concept to real SPY data, showing that while SPY has a clear upward trend, its distance from a rolling mean exhibits mean-reverting behavior. He presents a simple Z-score based strategy coded in Python, which achieved approximately 12% growth over a period, outperforming buy-and-hold when filtered by regime. He concludes by inviting viewers to comment 'OU' to receive the notebook, emphasizing that understanding mean reversion helps identify better entry points.

- **Fecha:** 2026-10-04
- **URL:** https://www.instagram.com/reel/DeD3ruoCXGg/
- **Likes:** 8000 | **Comentarios:** 38
- **Detalle:** An educational screen-recorded tutorial explains Harry Markowitz's Efficient Frontier by simulating 5,000 portfolio mixes of three assets in Python and showing how the upper edge represents the maximum return for each level of risk.

Narrativa: This is Part 13 of 'Quant Finance From Scratch,' titled 'The Nobel Prize Curve' / 'The Efficient Frontier.' The narrator explains Harry Markowitz's concept that shows the best possible return for every level of risk. Using Python, the video simulates three assets: A (like bonds) at 4% return and 6% risk, B (like stocks) at 7% return and 18% risk, and C (riskier stock) at 11% return and 25% risk, with assumed correlations. It then generates 5,000 random portfolio mixes by weighting these assets, plotting each mix as a dot on a risk (x-axis) vs. expected return (y-axis) map. At every level of risk, the program keeps only the mix with the highest return, forming the upper edge of the cloud of dots — the Efficient Frontier. Key findings shown: the lowest-risk point on the frontier achieves 5.7% risk with 4.4% return, better than asset A alone; at B's risk level (18%), the frontier offers 9.1% return versus B's 7%, improving the Sharpe ratio from 0.22 to 0.34. The video concludes by summarizing the method: mix assets in every way, plot them, and keep the top mix at each risk level.

Slide 1 defines the Null Hypothesis as the default assumption of no real edge. Slide 2 explains P-Value as the odds a result happened by chance. Slide 3 illustrates Type I Error (finding a false edge) and Type II Error (missing a real edge). Slide 4 covers T Tests as a basic filter checking if average returns differ from zero. Slide 5 details the Multiple Testing Problem, showing how testing many strategies yields false positives by luck alone. Slide 6 contrasts a smooth Backtest with volatile Live Trading, highlighting the Overfitting Gap and Execution Costs, noting most backtests fail live. Slide 7 is promotional, showing a video call and announcing the launch of the Quant Finance Bootcamp to upskill concepts, with a call to follow. Narrativa: A man with glasses and a dark long-sleeve shirt sits at a wooden table and presents a tiered ranking of technical market indicators, displayed on screen from S to F. He places MACD in F tier, calling it a lagging indicator that was late by an average of 11 days in the 2022 bear market. Simple Moving Averages (50 and 200-day) are ranked A tier because institutions like pension funds, CTAs, and hedge funds watch them. Stochastics is D tier, described as MACD with more noise that whipsaws in choppy markets. Fibonacci retracement is B tier, working because enough people believe in it despite no economic reason. RSI is C tier, measuring momentum but often staying overbought or oversold for weeks, such as crude oil in 2022. Bollinger Bands are B tier for measuring volatility. Donchian Channels are A tier for breakout systems, showing when price does something it hasn't done in 20 days. Volume is S tier, called the only non-derived indicator that shows conviction and positioning before price moves. He notes he uses these indicators in his Crown Macro research and invites viewers to comment 'LETTER' for a newsletter link, with a disclaimer that it is educational only and not investment advice.

## 141. rational_edge - 2026-07-15
- **ID:** post-660981079370791775705
- **Autor:** Rational Edge (@rational_edge)
- **Fecha:** 2026-07-15
- **URL:** https://www.instagram.com/reel/Da0BA-GRGN1/
- **Likes:** 4600 | **Comentarios:** 71
- **Detalle:** Educational video explaining how Fourier transforms create false cycles in random-walk price data due to endpoint mismatch, warning quants not to mistake artifacts for tradable edges.

Narrativa: The video is an educational explainer about the misuse of Fourier analysis in financial markets, posted by @rational_edge. Set against a dark interface with the title 'FOURIER', it demonstrates that 'every chart is a sum of circles.' Using a constructed random walk with zero true cycles, it shows how 8 spinning vectors can redraw the price path. The Discrete Fourier Transform then reports a dominant '512-day cycle' holding 39% of power. The video explains this is an artifact: the DFT assumes the series repeats forever, creating a 68-point cliff between the end (31.5) and start (100) prices. That discontinuity is a sawtooth wave composed of low-frequency harmonics. Removing the linear ramp so endpoints match causes 57% of the low-harmonic amplitude to disappear. On a log-log plot, the true structure emerges: price power follows ~k^-1.92 (random walk) and returns are flat (~white noise). Fitting 128 harmonics achieves perfect in-sample fit (R²=1.000) but forecasts 2.84x worse than 'tomorrow = today'. Testing 2,000 random walks, 98.7% falsely show a multi-year cycle. The core message: the cycle you find is often just the cliff between your last bar and your first.

The visual demonstration uses a daily candlestick: he draws a purple box from the prior day's high to low. He then switches to a 5-minute chart inside that box. The rule is simple: if price reaches the top of the box, look to sell; if it reaches the bottom, look to buy; if it's in the middle, do nothing. He argues this is how 'smart money' trades. For entries, he adds confluence: at the top, wait for one red candle to close below the previous candle, then short with a stop just above that red candle's high; at the bottom, wait for one green candle to close above the previous, then long with a stop just below its low. He emphasizes the simplicity helps generate daily gains, reiterating the P/L figure, and ends by telling viewers to save the video. The on-screen caption states 'This took me 26 years to learn.
